Crane Performance

Round 8: Crane beside Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Lightpanda, the same pages in every engine, CPU and memory. Crane is its release build, ReleaseSafe; a ReleaseFast build runs beside it as a column of its own. Only Crane ran in this round; the browsers’ rows are round 0’s (a browser runs again when it updates). Crane is built for one fixed CPU, apple_m1-fuse_aes, never its build host’s. Measured on Apple M4 Pro, 14 cores, macOS 27.2 (26B123). WPT results.

Crane against each browser

Crane’s time and memory as a percentage of the browser’s: the geometric mean, over the workloads both engines ran, of Crane’s figure divided by the browser’s. Below 100% Crane uses less. Start-up and the harness’s own floor are rows of their own below, not in these figures.

Progress

Crane over time

Crane’s own time and memory in each measurement round as a share of round 0’s: the geometric mean, over the workloads both rounds ran, of each workload’s figure over its round-0 figure (memory is the steady footprint). Round 0 is 100%; lower is better.

From round 1 Crane is built for one fixed CPU target, apple_m1-fuse_aes; re-measuring round 0’s source for it changed time by 0.991× and memory by 1.010×, within noise (both within 5%).

  • Round 0, 2026-11-06, Crane 9b0f1b1f382 for apple_m4-fuse_aes (native), the reference (100%): The baseline: the suite's first measurement, of main 9b0f1b1f382, built for its build host's CPU.
  • Round 1, 2026-11-06, Crane 0b6101f5f65 for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 81.2% and memory 73.4% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): Lane perf1: DOM memory reported to V8, and a garbage tree's deferred teardown weighed by its nodes. Lane perf2: getElementsBy* return one live list per document, and querySelector, matches and closest parse through a per-document selector cache with Blink's ID path.
  • Round 2, 2026-11-07, Crane d1ee79a9386 for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 56.3% and memory 57.5% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): Lane perf2: each node type's state in its own generated State, read with no hash lookup; a generated State that holds only its internal pointers (a kept div 4,683 -> 2,782 B); NodeBase 472 -> 208 B, and every block a node holds reported to V8. Lane perf3: a DOM call's values are V8 Locals - no Global per getter, setter or method call - and a Global's dispose takes no lock. Lane anim1: CSS transitions and animations from style.
  • Round 3, 2026-11-08, Crane 9053db756ef for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 52.6% and memory 40.4% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): Lane perf2: one block per node (a div's five arena requests become one) in exact size classes; NodeBase 208 -> 88 B (the sibling links are the child list, and the per-node allocator copies are gone); Document rare data. Lane perf3: an element's attributes out of line in an exact-size list, ElementRareData made on first use, and the custom element registry association as two bits (Element state 848 -> 136 B). By this page's own measure a kept div 2,259 -> 782 B.
  • Round 4, 2026-11-09, Crane cea6ce9cae5 for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 52.9% and memory 40.2% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): No size phase, and flat against round 3 as expected: the wrapper redesign's groundwork (lane wr1: a node's wrapper slot as an engine-protocol type, with its identity tests; lane wr2: a cppgc heap per agent, used by nothing yet) and lane perf2's byte-by-byte breakdown of a kept node.
  • Round 5, 2026-11-09, Crane 8fc81822751 for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 51.8% and memory 40.0% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): Lane wr1, the wrapper redesign's second phase: a node's wrapper leaves the wrapper cache - a weak handle constructed in the node's own slot, and an 8-B cell in a per-agent registry. A kept div 778 -> 546 B, a text node 637 -> 401 B.
  • Round 6, 2026-11-11, Crane 253917157d7 for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 49.9% and memory 36.6% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): The node-data lanes. nd1: a CharacterData node's data 48 -> 16 B. nd2: an element's name is one pointer to a shared record, its attributes one block of 8-B entries whose values are interned, counted records, and a class value's token list is made once per value. rare1: one sparse rare block per element. lis1: a target's listeners and handlers in one lazily made block. cg1: [SameObject] caches in the rare block, and a generated State keeps only the slots its impl uses. style1: a per-document matched-properties cache. h1: the per-Browser address-space reservation. A kept div 546 -> 368 B.
  • Round 7, 2026-11-13, Crane e7024f71f0b for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 45.0% and memory 37.7% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): Lane v8up: V8 13.1.203 -> 15.7.112.43, the V8 Chrome 157 ships, with wrapper finalizers run at binding exits and inside callbacks; Crane's builds take the V8 adapter's default build options, a 24 MB young generation (8 MB semispaces) and a collection after a main frame's navigation once V8's heap holds 16 MB. Lane nd2: equal attribute lists share one block at parse and clone, through a cache their Document owns. Lane style1: a style's own blocks keyed by content in the matched-properties cache. Against round 6's build run again in this round's window (the drift control), time fell by more than 5% on 31 of 45 workloads and rose on none, and the pages that navigate hold 0.61-0.82x the memory. Memory rose by more than 5% on 18 workloads, 2.8% over the suite: V8 15.7 allocates a cppgc object per wrapper (the attribute and Event-dictionary rows 1.17x; a kept div 368 -> 386 B), a detached tree waits one collection longer (cloneNode 1.39x, document.write 1.25x), and 15.7 collects later than 13.1 did (fetch over loopback 1.31-1.47x, streams.reader 1.41x, each further instance 1.12x). Accepted for this round by the user's ruling of 2026-11-12, each row queued with an owner (lanes wrap1, wr1 and v8up).
  • Round 8, 2026-11-13, Crane f91f0cce497 for apple_m1-fuse_aes, time 43.1% and memory 35.4% of round 0’s (over 45 and 47 workloads): Lane wr1, the rest of the wrapper redesign (phases P4-P8): a node's wrapper has no internal field - it reaches its node through an anchor on V8's C++ heap (v8::Object::Wrap) - and the node holds it by a reference V8 may drop in a young collection once script holds no reference to the wrapper and has added nothing to it; what a kept wrapper's tree keeps alive is found by a walk in the collector's pause; a collected wrapper's hold on its node is released right after the collection that found it dead; and V8 is told 8 B for each dropped wrapper, so its own limit paces full collections under churn. V8 starts with --no-handle-weak-ref-weakly-in-minor-gc, so a FinalizationRegistry callback does not fire for a node still in its document. Against round 7's build run again in this round's window (the drift control): time 0.975x, instructions 0.985x, memory 0.937x; memory fell by more than 5% on 12 of 47 workloads and rose by that much on none (querySelectorAll, matches and getElementsByClassName 0.65x; cloneNode 0.75x, which takes back round 7's 1.39x; the tree page's other rows 0.82-0.83x; createElement and the getter, setter and method loops 0.91-0.92x), and time fell by more than 5% on 8 of 45 and rose on none (insert-remove 0.74x, build-tree 0.82x). A kept div 386 -> 336 B; a div in the document, whose wrapper script let go, 397 -> 294 B; a div with two attributes 437 -> 385 B; a text node 374 -> 306 B; a parsed span, which never had a wrapper, stays at 489 B. Stated as they are, not as noise: cloneNode retires 1.046x the instructions between its marks (a dropped clone's frees now run inside them; lane wr1 measured 0.885x through the settle mark); load.news holds 1.049x at its last reading and load.app peaks at 1.16x, because the collection after a navigation lands a page earlier or later than it did - one reading after page 10 of a sawtooth (owner: lane v8up); the Event and attribute rows read one of two levels from run to run, 0.84-0.86x in 3 runs of 7 and 0.96-0.98x in 4, and the median shown is the higher: each of the 650,000 Events a workload drops leaves 32 B until the next full collection, and the level is whether one lands inside the page (owner: lane wrap1, the non-node wrappers); and the rows where V8 15.7 keeps memory for 8-30 s after the work, as Chrome does (fetch over loopback, streams.reader, each further instance), are unchanged.

Crane 1.2×		259 B
Crane 1.2×		182 B
Crane 2.7×

How: one case per page (tests/perf/elements.json), each page in a fresh launch, so memory one case freed cannot make another’s read smaller. A page first makes everything but the nodes - a slot for each in an array, a host element, the markup to parse - so both readings hold it. The runner reads the footprint (phys_footprint, summed over the engine’s processes, as everywhere on this page) when the page reports, and the page reports after a full collection it asks for (TestUtils.gc() twice in Crane and Crane ReleaseFast; gc() twice in Chrome, launched with --js-flags=--expose-gc) and then 3.0 s idle; Firefox, Safari and Lightpanda let no page ask for a collection, so their readings come after the idle time alone. Each figure is the median over the runs (5 each), with the runs’ median absolute deviation beside it where that is 5% or more. The JS heap is Chrome’s performance.memory.usedJSHeapSize (launched with --enable-precise-memory-info), the only engine here that tells a page its heap. In the engines that render - Chrome, Firefox and Safari - a node in the document also gets a layout object and a computed style, so their figures for the nodes in the document and the parsed ones include rendering, which Crane and Lightpanda do not do; the nodes kept in an array, outside the document, are the DOM alone in every engine. Firefox’s footprint moved by -30.5 MB between the two readings of the page that made nothing (the median over its runs), which its figures have subtracted, so they carry that drift’s spread.

Its time was 98.3% and its memory 100.4% of round 7’s (geometric means over 45 and 47 workloads): within noise (both means within 5% of 100%; 0 workloads outside their own band of 10% or three times the runs’ spread). How it is measured

Every engine opens the same pages from tests/perf/, served from loopback with cross-origin isolation (so every engine’s clock is at its finest), each launch a fresh process with a fresh profile, the engines in alternating order on one quiet host. A page times each workload itself (performance.now() around a fixed number of operations, after warm-up runs) and reports through the benchmark server, which reads the engine’s whole process set when each mark arrives: CPU time and instructions between a workload’s first and last mark, phys_footprint (Activity Monitor’s Memory) at each, and the peak a 50 ms sampler saw between them. Start-up - from launch to the first page’s load - is its own row, so no other row includes it. Crane is its release build: ReleaseSafe throughout, libc malloc, V8 linked statically; rendering and layout are not Crane’s, so no workload measures them. The full method is in docs/performance.md.

Crane ReleaseFast is that build with only the optimize mode changed (-Dwpt-runner-release-fast: ReleaseFast throughout, no C sanitizer, the same V8 and snapshot), launched the same way and alternating with the other engines in the same runs.

Every Crane build is compiled for one fixed CPU, apple_m1-fuse_aes (-Dcpu), never its build host’s: two builds of one source for two CPUs are two code layouts, and a sub-microsecond row can move about 15% between them. The browsers ship one build for every Apple Silicon Mac. The build records its target, every row carries it, and the runner refuses to compare Crane builds made for different CPUs.

Written as HTML by tools/perf_bench from the benchmark journals (docs/performance.md).
